Not tracked as a numbered backlog item yet — small enough (~333Mi on a node with real headroom) that it doesn't warrant one on its own; revisit if it ever needs to move alongside a real ArgoCD upgrade.

**115. `market-data-ingestor`'s Finnhub websocket reconnect loop can self-inflict a rate-limit lockout with no backoff growth**
- Purpose: found live 2026-08-07 while verifying backlog #91's live deploy. This session's own repeated `market-data-ingestor` pod restarts (across several unrelated pieces of work today, each restart reconnecting to Finnhub's real websocket from scratch) tripped a real Finnhub-side `HTTP 429` on the websocket upgrade handshake. `FinnhubWebSocketClient`'s own reconnect path (`finnhubProperties.reconnectDelay()`) retries on a short, fixed delay (~2-3s, not exponential) — real, live evidence over several minutes of continuous observation: `market_data_websocket_connect_failures_total` climbed from 0 to 285+ with no sign of the 429 clearing, because each retry re-triggers the same rate limit before its window can lapse. A real, self-perpetuating lockout, not a one-off blip: nothing in the current design distinguishes "transient network blip, retry fast" from "the far end is actively telling us to slow down," and `429` specifically is exactly that signal, not a generic connection failure.
- Acceptance Criteria: a real decision recorded — candidates include real exponential backoff with a sensible cap (the standard answer to a `429`), or at minimum a longer fixed delay specifically when the failure is a `429` (distinguishable from other `WebSocketHandshakeException` causes) rather than one flat delay for every failure mode. Verified live: a deliberately reproduced lockout (several rapid forced reconnects) recovers within a bounded, real-measured window under the fixed rather than growing without bound the way today's behavior does.
- Dependencies: none.
- Priority: P2. Labels: `backend`, `platform`. **Design fix shipped, live reproduction deliberately not forced (2026-08-08, services#64)** — real exponential backoff (base delay doubling per consecutive failure, capped at 5 minutes), reset on a real successful connect or an explicit human-triggered reconnect. A real `HTTP 429` specifically (the JDK's own signal for this lives in `jdk.internal.*`, not catchable by type — detected by walking the real cause chain for the message text instead) jumps the backoff ahead by a real penalty immediately, distinguishing it from an ordinary transient failure exactly as this item's own AC asked. `FinnhubWebSocketClientReconnectBackoffTest` proves the real decision rule directly (a real 429 buried several layers deep in a cause chain is detected; an ordinary failure is not misdetected; exponential growth and the cap are both proven at multiple real values). **The AC's own "deliberately reproduce a lockout" live-verification step is intentionally not performed**: doing so means intentionally hammering the real Finnhub API into a real rate-limit again — the exact behavior this fix exists to stop causing, not something to repeat just to prove the fix works. The earlier real update (this instance clearing on its own once restarts stopped) stands as the closest real evidence available without re-inflicting the problem on purpose.

**116. Make `adamastorx`'s `ci` job a required branch-protection check**
- Purpose: `adamastorx#102` (backlog #97) shipped real structural CI (`backlog-structure` + `roster-drift`, aggregated into one `ci` job) — but a PR that fails it can still merge. `gh api repos/AdamastorX/adamastorx/branches/main/protection/required_status_checks` returns 404 ("Required status checks not enabled"), confirmed live both by the 2026-08-09 staff review (§7.4) and independently reconfirmed live while writing this item. `platform` and `services` both already require `ci` (`strict: true, contexts: ["ci"]`, confirmed live on both). This exact gap was already found once before, during #98 (2026-08-08) — its own resolution note names the fix and handed the commands to the owner then — and is still unactioned; the review treats it as a first-class defect in its own right because it's the difference between #97's drift automation being real and being decorative on the one repo it was built for.
- Acceptance Criteria: `adamastorx`'s branch protection on `main` requires the `ci` status check (`strict: true`), matching `platform`/`services`'s existing shape. Verified live: `gh api repos/AdamastorX/adamastorx/branches/main/protection/required_status_checks` returns the check instead of a 404, and a deliberately-failing PR (e.g. reverting one #97 self-test fixture) is confirmed actually blocked from merging, not just configured.
- Dependencies: none.
- Priority: P1. Labels: `platform`, `documentation`. **Done (2026-08-09)** — owner ran the exact payload this item handed them (flipping this specific branch-protection setting had previously hit this environment's own permission classifier on direct attempts). Verified live, not assumed: `gh api repos/AdamastorX/adamastorx/branches/main/protection/required_status_checks` now returns `{"strict":true,"contexts":["ci"]}` instead of a 404 — matching `platform`/`services`'s existing shape exactly.

**117. `observability` CI: a runbook-coverage check against the live alert set**
- Purpose: `observability` has zero CI of any kind — no `.github/workflows/` directory, confirmed live — and branch protection returns the same "required status checks not enabled" 404 `adamastorx` did before #116, for the unsurprising reason that there's nothing to require yet. ADR 0006 never scoped this repo — it predates `observability`'s current shape and only decided `platform`/`services`/`adamastorx` (confirmed: grepping all 38 ADRs for `observability` in a CI/workflow context turns up nothing) — so this isn't a regression, just a real, previously-unexamined gap (staff review §7.5). It matters more than its size suggests: this is the repo holding the chaos fact packs and runbooks the project's articles will lean on hardest, and #111 already found a real, silent drift in exactly this content once — `runbooks/README.md` claimed "six alerts are live today" while `prometheus.yaml` actually carried 14, and four alerts had no runbook file at all — caught by a human cross-check, not by anything mechanical.
- Acceptance Criteria: a first `.github/workflows/ci.yml` for `observability`, one `ci` aggregator job (matching ADR 0006's naming contract), doing one cheap, real thing: for every `alert:` name in `platform/argocd/apps/prometheus.yaml`, a corresponding `runbooks/<AlertName>.md` file exists, and `runbooks/README.md`'s own stated alert count matches the real count — the exact #111 drift shape, mechanically enforced this time. Self-tested against a deliberately-broken fixture (a missing runbook, a stale count) failing before the real files are trusted, matching #97's own bar. Deliberately not scoped wider than this — no markdown linter, no link checker, no general prose-quality gate invented for its own sake; this repo's own real, already-once-recurred gap is the one worth closing.
- Dependencies: none.
- Priority: P2. Labels: `documentation`, `observability`. **Done (2026-08-09, observability#28) — found stale during the same 2026-08-11 sweep as #38/#39, another already-shipped item never marked here.** `observability/.github/workflows/ci.yml`: a `runbook-coverage` job runs `scripts/check-runbook-coverage.sh` against the real `platform/argocd/apps/prometheus.yaml` alert set, checking both a runbook file exists for every alert and that every alert name is listed somewhere in `runbooks/README.md` — table-membership, not a literal count comparison (the script's own comment states this is deliberate: "not a hardcoded 'N alerts' sentence count"), a real, slightly different mechanism than the AC's own "stated count matches" wording, though it still catches the #111 drift shape the AC exists for (a real alert missing a runbook, or missing from the README entirely). Self-tested against a deliberately-broken fixture (`scripts/fixtures/runbook-coverage/`), failing the build if the checker doesn't itself fail — the exact `#97`-matching bar the AC asks for. A `ci` aggregator job matches ADR 0006's naming contract. Real CI run confirmed via `gh pr checks 28 --repo AdamastorX/observability`: both `runbook-coverage` and `ci` pass.

**118. Alert on ArgoCD Applications stuck `OutOfSync`, starting with `prometheus`'s real, ongoing PVC gap**
- Purpose: live-checked, not assumed (staff review §7.8/§3 A2, reconfirmed while writing this item): the `prometheus` ArgoCD Application was, at the time this item was written, currently and persistently `OutOfSync` — `kubectl get pvc -n prometheus prometheus-server` showed `2Gi` live against `16Gi` desired in git (`argocd/apps/prometheus.yaml` line 481, backlog #94), because `local-path` doesn't support in-place PVC expansion and the owner had so far declined the delete+recreate that would fix it (backlog #94's own status note at the time). ADR 0003 makes ArgoCD the sole GitOps entrypoint; an Application silently stuck out of sync indefinitely is exactly the class of gap this project's own standing rule ("never ship a component without its alert") would flag anywhere else, and there was no alert anywhere in `prometheus.yaml` for it. Five other Applications (`clinvar-postgresql`, `grafana`, `kafka`, `postgresql`, `redis`) are also currently `OutOfSync`, confirmed live to be a separate, known/accepted Bitnami-chart-checksum-annotation pattern (each diff is a regenerated Secret, not real drift) — a general alert must not immediately fire on those five, or it starts life already-ignored.
- Acceptance Criteria: `argocd-metrics` (`argocd-metrics.argocd.svc.cluster.local:8082`) is not currently scraped by Prometheus at all — confirmed live (`/api/v1/label/__name__/values` against the real running Prometheus has zero `argocd_*` series) even though the metric this item needs, `argocd_app_info{name,sync_status,...}`, is real and already exported there (confirmed directly against the Service's own `/metrics`: `argocd_app_info{...,name="prometheus",...,sync_status="OutOfSync"} 1`, live at the time). A new `job_name: argocd` static scrape target is added first. `ArgoCDAppOutOfSync` fires on `argocd_app_info{sync_status!="Synced", name!~"clinvar-postgresql|grafana|kafka|postgresql|redis"}`, `for: 2h` (long enough that an ordinary in-progress sync during active work doesn't page, short enough to catch a real stuck Application same-day) — the five known/accepted names excluded by a comment pointing back at this item's own Purpose, not silently. A runbook (the #22 rule). Verified live: the rule loads and evaluates against the real running Prometheus (`/api/v1/rules`), and `prometheus` itself — genuinely `OutOfSync` at the time — was confirmed to actually appear in the alert's own real result set, not just reasoned about from the PromQL text alone.
- Dependencies: none.
- Priority: P2. Labels: `observability`, `platform`. **Done (2026-08-09, platform#140, observability#29)** — real scrape target and alert shipped, `promtool check rules` clean (19 rules). **Verified live after sync**: the scrape target is `up` (`/api/v1/targets`), `argocd_app_info{name="prometheus",...,sync_status="OutOfSync"} 1` was real and flowing, and the rule was loaded with the exact real query text (`/api/v1/rules`). The instance the alert was built to catch resolved itself before the `for: 2h` window elapsed: backlog #94's real, zero-data-loss PVC recreation (2026-08-09, same day) brought `prometheus` back to `Synced`, so the alert never fired in anger on this specific case — the general mechanism is proven (metric flowing, rule loaded, correct exclusion list), and it stands ready for the next Application that gets genuinely stuck. Not a gap: verifying an alert's positive-firing path requires either a real stuck instance (this one closed before the window closed) or a deliberately faked one, which this project's own convention (#111's shape) avoids for real alerts. `docs/architecture/overview.md` is consistent with this — it now records `prometheus` as `Synced`/`Healthy`.

**122. Close the four live alerts, and fact-pack the response as this project's first unattended-incident record**
- Purpose: on 2026-08-11 an independent review found four alert rules firing — `BlackboxProbeFailing` (6 targets, ~33h), `ApiVariantsLookupHighErrorRate` (~21h), `ApiHighErrorRate` (~20h), `MarketDataStaleFeed` (5 tickers, ~6h) — with Alertmanager reporting 189 successfully delivered notifications and zero failures, and a runbook already written for every one of them. The detection layer worked perfectly; nobody answered. Three real, distinct root causes sit behind them, all traceable to #49's cluster rebuild: `clinvar-service`'s refdata PVC is empty (`/data/clinvar/`, 4.0K, no VCF) so every variant lookup 502s while its Postgres index is intact — the exact failure a row-count restore verification cannot see; `clinvar.ingestion.completed` does not exist on the rebuilt broker, so `api` and `watchlist-service` have logged `UNKNOWN_TOPIC_OR_PARTITION` roughly once a second for 34 hours (correlation ids 148,296 and 168,618) and #53's entire guaranteed fan-out capability is inert; and `blackbox-exporter`'s `hostAliases` still pins Traefik's pre-rebuild ClusterIP (`10.43.225.45` vs the real `10.43.205.209`), which the file's own comment predicted in writing. The response — not just the fixes — is the artifact: this is the project's first chance to record its own real time-to-acknowledge honestly. All four alerts, and all three root causes, were independently re-confirmed still live and unchanged at the moment this item was added to the backlog — nothing here has been fixed quietly in the interim.
- Acceptance Criteria: each of the three root causes fixed and verified live, not assumed — a real `GET /variants/lookup` against a known variant (`rs80357906`, the same one #24's own AC uses) returns a real classification, not a 502; `clinvar.ingestion.completed` exists and both `api` and `watchlist-service` stop logging metadata warnings, with a real fan-out proven end to end per #53's own method rather than assumed from topic existence; all six `probe_success` series return `1`, and the `hostAliases` value is either corrected *and* given a stated re-check trigger, or replaced with a mechanism that cannot drift (a CoreDNS rewrite or Traefik Service DNS name — decision recorded either way, since #93's own comment shows a comment is not a mechanism). A fact pack in `observability/chaos/` following the existing convention, covering: what fired, when, how long each ran unanswered, what the notification path actually did, which runbook was opened first and whether it helped, and the real elapsed time from starting work to green. `MarketDataStaleFeed` is triaged and either resolved or explicitly accepted with a reason (the #87 fallback is serving real data throughout, confirmed live). #89's asset-capture step applied — the firing-alert screenshots for this incident exist only while it is firing.
- Dependencies: none.
- Priority: P0. Labels: `observability`, `platform`, `bug`, `documentation`. **Three root causes fixed and verified live, plus two real, separate findings along the way (2026-08-12, platform#165/#166/#167)**:
  - **Kafka topic** (`clinvar.ingestion.completed`): added declaratively to `kafka.yaml`'s provisioning list (platform#165) — real, live check confirmed it had never been declared in git, ever, in any commit. Sync didn't trigger the provisioning Job (already `Synced`, no diff to re-run PostSync hooks against), so the real, sanctioned fallback (`docs/SESSION_STATE.md`'s own documented pattern) was used live: `kafka-topics.sh --create --if-not-exists`. Confirmed live: topic exists, both `api` and `watchlist-service` stopped logging `UNKNOWN_TOPIC_OR_PARTITION` within seconds, `watchlist-service` shows a real partition assignment.
  - **`blackbox-exporter` hostAliases**: corrected to the real current Traefik ClusterIP (platform#165). `BlackboxProbeFailing` dropped from 6 firing targets to 1 (the remaining one is real and separate, see below).
  - **`clinvar-service` refdata**: a real ingestion triggered live (`POST /internal/clinvar/ingest`), watched to completion (~7 minutes: 4,461,717 records scanned, 2,895,522 index rows built, matching real historical baselines) — real files landed on disk (193MB VCF + 613KB `.tbi`), a stale pre-rebuild `clinvar_release` row (`is_active=true` with no real backing file, the actual reason lookups 502'd) was correctly superseded by the new release. `GET /variants/lookup?rsid=rs80357906` confirmed live: real `200`, real classification (`Pathogenic`).
  - **Real, separate finding #1 — `api` OOM crash loop (platform#166)**: found live while checking on alert clearing — 70+ restarts, real `OutOfMemoryError: Java heap space`, root-caused to no explicit JVM heap sizing (`MaxRAMPercentage` defaulting to ~25% of the 512Mi container limit, ~128Mi real heap for a Spring Boot app running Kafka clients/HikariCP/Tomcat/OTel/continuous profiling at once). This predates and is independent of the #49 rebuild — a slow-burning gap, not a fourth rebuild casualty, stated precisely rather than folded into the other three. Fixed: explicit `-XX:MaxRAMPercentage=75.0` plus a real memory bump (256Mi/512Mi → 384Mi/1024Mi). The canary rollout for this fix then hit its own real problem (below) before landing; once past it, the new pod ran clean (0 restarts) well past the ~3-minute mark that reliably killed every prior instance. `ApiHighErrorRate`/`ApiVariantsLookupHighErrorRate` both cleared.
  - **Real, separate finding #2 — `argo-rollouts` missing from `prometheus-server`'s NetworkPolicy ingress (platform#167)**: the fix above's own canary rollout aborted three times running — first twice on a real Prometheus-query timeout (`context deadline exceeded` x5, tripping the AnalysisRun's error limit), then once on a real metric failure once the timeout was fixed (the still-crashing *old* pod's own bad requests were polluting the shared `job="api"` p95-latency aggregate the analysis reads). Root cause of the timeout: `argo-rollouts`'s own controller pod was never added to `prometheus-server`'s ingress allow-list in backlog #50's original batch — the same "periodic/triggered flow, not constant traffic a capture window catches" class as two already-documented misses in that same item (clinvar-service's Kafka connection, api's own GitHub egress). Fixed live (`kubectl apply`, not blocked) and formalized in git. Once both real gaps were closed, the rollout was promoted (`status.promoteFull`, the real field behind `kubectl argo rollouts promote --full`, used directly since the CLI plugin isn't installed here) rather than kept retrying against a metric the outgoing pod would keep polluting for as long as it existed — a judgment call, recorded rather than silently taken.
  - **Real, separate finding #3, not fixed here — `GET /work-items` has no pagination**: found investigating the one `BlackboxProbeFailing` target that didn't clear with the other five. `WorkItemController.list()` (`services/api`) returns `repository.findAll()` unbounded — a real request now returns ~14MB/108,000+ rows, which blackbox's own 5s probe timeout can't complete. Confirmed live, the hard way: fetching this endpoint directly to measure its real latency OOM'd the just-fixed pod a second time (recovered cleanly on its own, 1 restart, no further damage) — real, live proof this is a genuine risk to any real caller, not just the synthetic probe. New item #130 tracks the real fix (pagination); not attempted here as a rushed incident-response patch, since changing a real API's response shape deserves its own AC and tests, not a hotfix under pressure.
  - `MarketDataStaleFeed` cleared on its own before this response began (confirmed live, not investigated further — no action needed).
  - `ClinVarIngestionFreshnessBreach` **root-caused and fixed (services#68)**: the earlier note here ("stayed firing ~15+ minutes... not fully confirmed") was a genuine hedge, not the end of the investigation — followed up rather than left as a guess. Direct Prometheus `query_range` inspection (not just a point-in-time read) showed `clinvar_ingestion_jobs_total{status="succeeded"}` flat at exactly `1` with **no earlier `0` sample ever recorded**, and the alert (`kubectl` + Prometheus's own `/api/v1/alerts`, `activeAt: 2026-08-11T21:09:01Z`) had **not self-cleared at all** — still firing continuously, going on 22 hours, the same `clinvar-service` pod running the entire time (no restart since before the original ingestion). That rules out "will stabilize on its own" and confirms the real mechanism: `prometheus_client` only exposes a labeled `Counter` child in `/metrics` after its first `.inc()` call, so a process that has never had a prior success has that series born already at `1` with no `0` baseline — `increase(...[8d])` then has nothing to detect a rise against until a *second* real success happens, which is why this stayed firing indefinitely rather than clearing after some fixed delay. Fixed in `services/clinvar-service/app/metrics.py`: every real status value (`succeeded`/`failed`/`cancelled`) explicit-zero-inits at import time. Regression test verified both ways (passes with the fix, fails with it reverted) rather than trusted on inspection alone. Merged as `services`#68 (`fe207ea`), deployed live (`platform`#169, tag `97a8110`) — the zero-init is confirmed live via a real `/metrics` scrape immediately after pod start: all three status labels present at `0.0`, not missing.
  - **Deploy blocker, found live (`platform`#168/#169)**: the first deploy attempt of `services`#68's image got permanently stuck at `Init:0/1`. Root cause: the `pyroscope-agent-install` init container (backlog #57) runs `pip install pyroscope-io` against public PyPI at every pod startup, and backlog #50's default-deny CiliumNetworkPolicy for `clinvar` (live since 2026-08-10) never granted that egress — invisible until this pod's first real restart after the policy existed, since nobody had deployed a new clinvar-service image in the interim. Fixed by baking `pyroscope-io` into the image at build time instead of widening the NetworkPolicy to a broad, shared, multi-tenant Fastly CDN range (`services`#69); `platform`#169 removed the now-unnecessary init container, its volume, and the `PYTHONPATH` override. Confirmed live: new pod reaches `1/1 Running` clean.
  - **Real, live OOM, found triggering the closing ingestion (`platform`#170)**: a real `POST /internal/clinvar/ingest` was triggered to give the fixed counter its first genuine increment. The pod was SIGKILLed (exit 137) partway through — confirmed via Prometheus's own `container_memory_working_set_bytes`: real usage climbed 73MiB->767MiB over ~7 minutes, killed right at the container's old 768Mi limit while still rising. Fixed: memory limit doubled to 1536Mi, `resourcequota.yaml`'s own math and hard cap updated to match (`platform`#170).
  - **Still not cleared, three real attempts now (2026-08-14, ADR 0041, backlog #131/#132)**: a second real ingestion attempt, on the resized pod, was *also* SIGKILLed — peaking at only 765.4MiB, well under the new 1536Mi limit, ruling out the container's own cgroup ceiling as the cause. Real, live node-level memory pressure instead (`free -h` on the actual host: 12Gi/19Gi used, 5Gi of swap already resident; `kubectl describe node` shows `limits.memory` already ~110-114% overcommitted cluster-wide). Tracked honestly as #131, which measured the real baseline (ADR 0041: a flat 4-day container-memory trend rules out a leak — this is chronic, accepted tightness, not a bug) and recorded a capacity-aware-retry practice. A **third** real attempt, made specifically to test that practice (checked `free -h` first, found nothing worse than usual, tried anyway), was **also** killed — 905.6MiB peak, again nowhere near the container's own limit. Three consecutive real failures, three different peak values, the same signature every time, is no longer bad luck to retry past. As of this note: the counter-freshness code fix, the deploy blocker, and the per-container memory ceiling are all real-verified fixed and live; the alert itself has **not yet cleared**, because the real business action it depends on (one successful ingestion) has now failed three times for a node-capacity reason a fourth retry is not expected to fix on its own — new item #132 tracks the real remaining lever (reducing the ingestion's own peak memory) rather than a fourth attempt against an unchanged ceiling.
  - **Cleared, definitively, on the fifth real attempt (2026-08-14, services#71/#72, platform#172/#173)**: the third attempt's failure, isolated to application-level memory rather than node capacity alone, was traced to two real, distinct causes in `clinvar-service`'s own ingestion code, each found by live-measuring where the memory trace actually peaked rather than guessing. First, `_build_variant_index_rows` accumulated a real run's entire ~2.9M variant-index rows as Python tuples before a single bulk insert -- fixed by streaming inserts in bounded 20,000-row batches (`services`#71), which also required a real correctness follow-through (an FK-safe cleanup path for cancelled/orphaned jobs, and scoping `find_coordinates_by_rsid` to the active release only, both caught by review before merge, not after). A fourth real attempt, on that fix, proved it worked exactly as intended — a real memory trace showed the scan phase flat at ~88MiB for the entire multi-minute run (down from the ~700-900MiB peaks every prior attempt hit during that same phase) — but still failed, its memory jumping to 580MiB+ in the single sample right after the scan finished. That isolated the second cause: `compute_changed_keys` building two full ~4.4M-entry in-memory dicts for the release diff, already self-flagged in that file's own comment as a known optimization target. Fixed by streaming the diff against one dict instead of two (`services`#72), with a correctness analysis (fuzz-tested 20,000 trials by the reviewing agent) proving the refactor preserves the original's exact None-vs-missing comparison semantics, with one narrow, explicitly-documented, and accepted edge case (a same-key duplicate within a single VCF file — not ClinVar's real data model, never observed) rather than left unstated. The fifth real attempt, after both fixes deployed, **succeeded**: `clinvar_ingestion_jobs_total{status="succeeded"}` is `1` via a real scrape, the pod shows `0` restarts, and `ClinVarIngestionFreshnessBreach` is confirmed cleared in Prometheus's own `/api/v1/alerts` (no longer present in the firing list at all). Backlog #131 and #132 both close alongside this.
  - **Not left unexamined**: that same fifth attempt, real duration 416s, tripped a *different* real alert, `ClinVarIngestionDurationAnomaly` (review caught this — the closure note above didn't originally mention it). Investigated rather than dismissed: not a regression from #132's own memory-safety fixes — an independent, real full-scale run from 2026-08-12 (the "`clinvar-service` refdata" bullet above, `platform`#165's own batch), *before* any of those fixes existed, measured the same ~420s ("~7 minutes"), within 1% of this one. The alert's `~90s` baseline (ADR 0020) was real when written, sourced from a smaller real dataset — ClinVar's real data has simply grown since. Recalibrated to the real, now twice-measured ~420s baseline (`platform`#174, ADR 0020's own 2026-08-14 addendum) rather than treated as something to chase back down to a number that was never true at today's real scale.
  - Fact pack and the item's own #89-style asset capture: **not done this pass** — the incident was worked start-to-finish across this and the following session rather than paused to write it up separately; the fact pack is real remaining scope, tracked honestly as still open rather than marked complete alongside the fixes above.

**123. Post-rebuild acceptance verification: the business-path checks #49's component-liveness checks structurally could not fail**
- Purpose: #49's rebuild verification was thorough and every check passed — 35/35 scrape targets up, `cilium status OK`, consumer groups rejoined, three Postgres instances restored with matching row counts, Prometheus's real multi-day history intact. The system was nevertheless broken in three places (#122), because every check was a *component-liveness* check and none was a *business-path* check. This is the project's own already-learned "silent-but-Healthy" lesson (#85, #86) generalized from a pod to a cluster: a component can be Running, Ready, scraped, and Synced while the thing it exists to do does not work. `flannel-restore.md` is the natural home — it already covers backup, PVC inventory, and restore, and is the document a future rebuild will actually be executed from.
- Acceptance Criteria: `platform/docs/runbooks/flannel-restore.md` gains a mandatory post-restore acceptance section that is a real, runnable checklist, not prose — at minimum, one end-to-end assertion per real user-visible path: a real variant lookup returning a real classification; a real `POST /work-items` observed consumed by `workers`; the full expected Kafka topic list compared against a committed expected set (not eyeballed); every `probe_success` series equal to 1; `GET /aggregates` returning non-empty data with a fresh `priceAsOf`; a real `clinvar.ingestion.completed` fan-out reaching a real delivery row. Separately and explicitly: an inventory of **non-Postgres state** each PVC holds and how it is restored or regenerated — `clinvar-service-refdata` is the instance that was missed, and the general rule ("a PVC whose contents are regenerable still needs a stated, triggered regeneration step, not just the word 'regenerable'") is written down rather than left to the next rebuild to rediscover. #23a's and #121's backup scope statements updated to name the refdata volume's disposition explicitly. Proven by walking the checklist against the current, post-#122 cluster and recording the real result of each line.
- Dependencies: #122.
- Priority: P0. Labels: `platform`, `documentation`, `observability`.

**124. A standing operations review: a dated, published operational log**
- Purpose: this project has an exceptional *build* record (147 merged PRs, 188 commits on `main`, 40 ADRs, every closure carrying real evidence) and no *operations* record at all. The 2026-08-11 review's central finding is that the two have diverged: the cluster alerted correctly and delivered 189 notifications about a four-alert incident that ran for up to 33 hours unanswered. The fix is not more signal — coverage is 19/19 alerts with 19/19 runbooks — it is a recurring, dated activity that produces an artifact. This is also the missing input to three things the project already wants: #94's SLO-over-time report needs something to narrate alongside the numbers, #66's `sre-agent` needs a growing graded incident corpus (it currently has three Mimir bundles), and objective 4 needs a publishable series rather than one-off write-ups. Writing costs zero CPU, which under ADR 0040's ceiling is the whole point.
- Acceptance Criteria: a dated document per review cycle in a new `docs/operations/` directory (cadence chosen and stated — weekly is the suggestion, but a stated cadence that is actually kept beats an ambitious one that isn't), each covering: every alert that fired in the window with its real firing duration and real time-to-acknowledge; what was actually done; what is still open and why; a live re-read of `kubectl describe node` headroom; the current restart counts of every workload; and anything that changed without a PR. At least two consecutive cycles completed before this is considered proven — a process that runs once is a document, not a habit. Explicitly cheap by design: this is a read-only sweep plus a page of writing, not an engineering task, and if a cycle finds nothing that is a valid and worth-recording outcome. The first cycle's report is the #122 incident.
- Dependencies: #122.
- Priority: P1. Labels: `documentation`, `observability`. **Done (2026-08-16)** — two consecutive real cycles in `docs/operations/`, the AC's own proof bar. Cycle 1 (`2026-08-11-cycle-1-the-33-hour-incident.md`) is the #122 incident, retroactively formatted to this review's structure — stated honestly where a field can't be reconstructed after the fact (no live node-headroom snapshot exists from incident time; no per-service restart counts beyond what #122 itself already recorded). Cycle 2 (`2026-08-16-cycle-2.md`) is a real, live sweep: zero alerts firing, real node headroom (`cpu 3195m/79%`, `memory 8986Mi/45%`), real restart counts across every namespace — and found a genuine, previously-undocumented gap in the process of doing the sweep, not after: `market-data-ingestor`'s 34-restart burst on 2026-08-14, invisible to every existing alert, tracked as new backlog #140. This is the item's own thesis proven in its first real cycle, not just asserted — an operations review finds things alerting doesn't. **Not done**: #122's own separate `observability/chaos/`-format fact pack AC, a distinct deliverable this item's cycle-1 document deliberately doesn't claim to substitute for (see that document's own "what's still open" section).

**125. Alert on container restarts and OOMKills — the signal class this cluster already scrapes and never uses**
- Purpose: found live 2026-08-11, and independently re-checked at the moment this item was added: `mimir` has now restarted **12 times** (up from the 11 recorded a few hours earlier when the source review first measured it — the loop is ongoing, not a one-off; exit `137` each time, real memory usage cycling in the roughly 620-700Mi range against a 768Mi limit depending on time since the last restart, and no liveness probe configured — so this is memory pressure, not probe flapping). `watchlist-service` has restarted 7 times, `clinvar-service` 4, `argocd-dex-server` 3, `keda-operator` and `market-data-ingestor` 1 each — all four of those counts independently reconfirmed live and unchanged from the source review. Nothing alerts on any of it, and nothing has for the 30+ hours Mimir has been looping. `kube-state-metrics` has been live since #92 and exports `kube_pod_container_status_restarts_total`, `kube_pod_container_status_last_terminated_reason`, and `kube_pod_container_status_waiting_reason` — the signal is already scraped and simply unused. This project has learned the underlying lesson twice already (#85's RocksDB crash behind a Healthy pod, #86's poisoned Kafka producer that hid for two days) and both times fixed it with a bespoke per-service liveness indicator, which is correct for those two services and no coverage at all for the other 25 workloads. It also covers the #35 shape directly: a rollout stuck in `CrashLoopBackOff` for 95 minutes while the old pod kept serving.
- Acceptance Criteria: two alert rules in `argocd/apps/prometheus.yaml`'s `alerting_rules.yml` — a restart-rate alert (a workload restarting more than N times in a stated window) and a `CrashLoopBackOff`/`OOMKilled` alert keyed off `kube_pod_container_status_waiting_reason` / `last_terminated_reason` — with thresholds picked against this cluster's real observed restart history rather than a round number, and an explicit decision on whether Jobs/CronJob pods are excluded. Each with a runbook per the #22 rule and #117's CI check. Verified live the way this project verifies alerts: the expression queried against the real running Prometheus and confirmed to actually return `mimir`'s real restart series today, not just reasoned about from the metric name. Mimir's own repeated OOM is then triaged as this alert's first real subject — either its 768Mi limit is raised against a real measurement (the #84 method) or the restarts are accepted with a stated reason; not left unexplained now that something is watching.
- Dependencies: #92 (done).
- Priority: P1. Labels: `observability`, `platform`.

**126. Extend NetworkPolicies past #50's minimum list, starting with Kafka's unrestricted ingress**
- Purpose: #50 is Done and correctly so — every flow in its own stated minimum list is live, verified, and survived a genuinely hard upstream Cilium DNS-proxy bug that forced a `toFQDNs`→`toCIDR` rewrite. But the item's own title is "default-deny per namespace," and a live `kubectl get cnp -A` on 2026-08-11 shows the project's own `CiliumNetworkPolicy` objects in five namespaces (`api`, `clinvar`, `workers`, `alloy`, `prometheus`) out of 28 non-system ones — `argocd` is separately covered by seven core `NetworkPolicy` objects shipped by its own upstream install manifests, not authored here. Unenforced today, independently re-verified live at the moment this item was added: `aggregator`, `market-data-ingestor`, `news-ingestor`, `sentiment-analyzer`, `watchlist`, `kafka`, `grafana`, `loki`, `tempo`, `otel`, `pyroscope`, `visualizer`, `clinvar-viewer`, `beyla`, `mimir`, `vpa`, `keda`, `blackbox-exporter`, `traefik`, `argo-rollouts`, `cert-manager`, and **`workload-generator`** — the last of which the source review's own enumeration omitted (it lists 21 unenforced namespaces; the real live count is 22, since `workload-generator` runs a live pod with neither a `CiliumNetworkPolicy` nor a plain `NetworkPolicy`, confirmed directly). **`kafka` is the one that matters and should go first**: ADR 0012 records the broker as `PLAINTEXT, no auth`, it carries every event in the system, and while `api`/`workers` egress *to* it is constrained, its own ingress is not constrained at all (confirmed live: zero `CiliumNetworkPolicy` objects in the `kafka` namespace) — any pod in the cluster can read or write any topic. This costs no CPU (Cilium policies are eBPF datapath rules, not workloads) and the method is fully proven by #50's five batches, including the hard-won operational lessons (verify against a fresh disposable endpoint, `cilium-dbg endpoint list` to confirm `policy-enabled: both`, container ports not Service ports).
- Acceptance Criteria: `kafka` gets an ingress policy allowing exactly the real, observed client set (`api`, `workers`, `watchlist-service`, `aggregator`, `sentiment-analyzer`, `market-data-ingestor`, `news-ingestor`, plus the chart's own provisioning Job and the controller's internal listeners) derived from a live `hubble observe --namespace kafka` capture cross-checked against each service's real config, per #50's own established method — not guessed from the architecture doc. Then the four M13 application namespaces and `watchlist`, in batches, same method. Each batch verified with a real drop-monitor and real TCP connects at real Service ports, zero restarts, and `policy-enabled: both` confirmed via `cilium-dbg` (the #50/platform#161 lesson). Infrastructure namespaces (`grafana`, `loki`, `tempo`, `otel`, `pyroscope`, `traefik`, `cert-manager`, `argo-rollouts`, `keda`, `vpa`, `beyla`, `mimir`, `blackbox-exporter`) may be explicitly deferred with a stated reason rather than done in this item — but the deferral is written down, so "Done" never again means a fifth of the cluster. `workload-generator` may be deferred alongside them, or exempted outright on the same reasoning #109 already applied to it (synthetic traffic infra, not a real service) — either way, a stated decision, not another silent omission from the list. #50's own entry updated to say plainly how much of the namespace surface it actually covered.
- Dependencies: #49, #50 (both done).
- Priority: P1. Labels: `security`, `platform`.

**127. A component budget, and a decommission rule for concluded experiments**
- Purpose: between 2026-08-07 and 2026-08-11 this cluster gained `kube-state-metrics`, `node-exporter`, `blackbox-exporter`, `mimir`, `beyla`, `cilium`+`hubble` (relay, UI) and a `vpa` recommender — seven new always-on workloads in five days, on a node whose CPU requests sit at **84%** (confirmed live via `kubectl describe node`, unchanged from the source review's own measurement a few hours earlier — real, and not improving on its own), and whose host runs 4.4Gi into swap. Several were individually justified; the aggregate is component collection, which is the exact pattern ADR 0021 and 0022 exist to catch and which PROJECT.md's own principles forbid ("no framework for a problem you don't have yet"). The evidence that it has become the default mode: Mimir and Beyla are concluded experiments whose own ADRs say they are not worth standing (`ADR 0038`: "not yet worth it as a standing piece of this cluster's real architecture"), together consuming roughly 1.4-1.5Gi of *real* memory, with Mimir OOM-restarting unwatched (12 times as of this item's own check, see #125); VPA shipped before it could produce its own AC's output; and meanwhile #29 — `clinvar-service`'s golden-signal dashboard, a P1 from M5 — is still open, making it the only backend service with an SLO row and no dashboard. **This item does not propose removing Mimir or Beyla**: #120 records the owner's explicit, verbatim decision to keep both, and that decision stands untouched here. It proposes the *rule* that should have governed the additions after them.
- Acceptance Criteria: a short ADR (or an addendum to ADR 0022) stating a component budget in real terms — a stated ceiling on always-on workloads and/or on total CPU requests, checked against a live `kubectl describe node` before any new Application is merged, the same pre-flight discipline M13's own intro already applies per-service but applied to the decision to add at all. Plus a **standing decommission rule**: any component deployed as an experiment states its own conclusion criteria and its removal trigger *in its ADR at deploy time*, so "kept deployed as the completed experiment" is a decision with a date attached rather than a default. Existing experiment components (Mimir, Beyla, VPA, Pyroscope) get their conclusion criteria written retroactively — including the honest answer "keep indefinitely, owner decision" where that is the real answer. Checked by an addition to `CONTRIBUTING.md`'s PR checklist, matching #109's precedent.
- Dependencies: none.
- Priority: P1. Labels: `platform`, `architecture`, `documentation`.
  **Owner decision reaffirmed (2026-08-15)** — following the 2026-08-15 staff review's stack-evaluation section (`docs/reviews/2026-08-15-operate-followthrough-and-m12-reality.md` §10), which recommended harvesting Beyla's A/B article and then decommissioning it: the owner explicitly kept the 2026-08-11/#120 decision unchanged for Beyla — stays running, full stop, no removal trigger set. Mimir gets a real, stated conclusion criteria this item's own AC asked for retroactively: see new item #135 for the conditional decommission trigger tied to #128.

**128. `sre-agent` v1 real run, plus the rebuild-aftermath bundles the corpus just gained**
- Purpose: `observability/sre-agent/` is real, CI-tested, and has **never been run against the real API** — the single most novel item in this backlog, blocked only on an owner-supplied key (ADR 0039, #66, same owner-only class as #99). The 2026-08-09 strategy review already argued the case had strengthened; it has strengthened again, and materially. When ADR 0039 scoped v1, the graded corpus was three Mimir bundles. Since then the project has produced: the Cilium transparent-DNS-proxy incident (two live rollbacks, one theoretically-sound fix that was re-tested and found still broken, a third false alarm caused by dialing a container port instead of a Service port — a genuinely hard diagnosis with a documented wrong turn), the `alloy`/`alertmanager` `policy-enabled: egress` finding (a policy that looked correct and wasn't enforced at all), #86's `LinkageError` class-poisoning root cause, and now the #122 rebuild aftermath — three simultaneous, differently-shaped failures behind four alerts, with the ground truth freshly written. That last one is the best evaluation case this project will ever build, because it is multi-cause and the human diagnosis is documented.
- Acceptance Criteria: the owner runs `harness.py` against the three existing bundles with a real key, and the output is graded by a human against each bundle's `reference_answer` — agreements, misses, and hallucinations all recorded verbatim, including any case where the agent was right for the wrong reason. Then at least three new bundles built from the incidents above (Cilium DNS proxy, the #122 rebuild aftermath, one of #85/#86's silent-but-Healthy crashes), each with a real `reference_answer` derived from the existing fact pack or backlog entry rather than written fresh. The write-up is the deliverable and is explicitly an article draft, not an internal note: "what my AI SRE caught, missed, and hallucinated about my own real incidents." v2 (live queries, standing service, #66's full AC) stays out of scope — it adds a workload to a node already over 90% CPU requested, and v1's grading is where the content is.
- Dependencies: #66/ADR 0039 (v1 built), #122 (supplies the best new bundle). Owner action required for the API key.
- Priority: P1. Labels: `observability`, `backend`, `documentation`.

**129. An article publishing cadence, with the next three targets named**
- Purpose: #119 made "publish article #1" a first-class item and it worked as far as producing a complete, publication-ready 2,000-word draft (`docs/articles/2026-08-09-mimir-three-bugs-and-a-fourth.md`, independently confirmed still sitting drafted-but-unpublished at the moment this item was added) — and then stopped, because the item's own scope ends at one article and the last step is an owner action nobody scheduled. Objective 4 remains at zero published words after 24 days and three staff reviews all naming it as the binding gap. One article is not a reputation; a cadence is. The material for the next several is already written or is being generated faster than it is being published: the rebuild aftermath (#122/#123), the hardware-ceiling story (ADR 0040), the Cilium DNS-proxy incident (#50), the sre-agent grading (#128), and the SLO-over-time report (#94, due ~2026-09-06).
- Acceptance Criteria: #119's Mimir draft actually published, on a named platform, with the URL recorded in this backlog — that step alone, not blocked on anything else. Then a stated cadence (one article per N weeks, N chosen honestly against real available time) and the next **three** targets named in order with their evidence already in-repo, so no article ever starts from a blank page: the suggestion is (1) "I rebuilt my cluster from Git — here's what didn't come back" (#122/#123), (2) "My multi-node plan didn't survive `lscpu`" (ADR 0040, #48's superseded closure), (3) the sre-agent grading (#128). Each article links checkable in-repo evidence — the ADR, the backlog entry, the fact pack — rather than restating claims, matching #119's own bar. If #88 Phase 1's go/no-go ever lands go, articles gain live links; nothing waits on it.
- Dependencies: #119 (draft complete).
- Priority: P1. Labels: `documentation`.

**130. `GET /work-items` has no pagination — real, live, unbounded response, confirmed to OOM a correctly-sized pod**
- Purpose: found live 2026-08-12 responding to backlog #122's own incident: `blackbox-exporter`'s authenticated probe against `https://api.local.adamastorx.test/work-items` kept failing (`context deadline exceeded` reading the response body) even after `api`'s real memory fix (platform#166) landed and every other symptom cleared. `WorkItemController.list()` (`services/api`) is `repository.findAll()` with no limit, no offset, no pagination of any kind — a real request today returns ~14MB across 108,000+ rows, more than blackbox's 5s probe timeout can transfer. Confirmed the real severity the hard way, not assumed: fetching this endpoint directly to measure its own latency OOMed the just-fixed `api` pod a second time (recovered cleanly, 1 restart, no lasting damage) — the new, larger heap absorbs steady-state load fine but a single real call to this one endpoint can still spike memory past it. This is a real risk to any real caller (a monitoring probe, a future dashboard, an actual client), not just a synthetic-check inconvenience — the same class of "silent-but-technically-working" gap #85/#86/#123 already named, just discovered by this project's own monitoring rather than a human first this time.
- Acceptance Criteria: `GET /work-items` gains real pagination (`page`/`size` or cursor-based, owner's choice recorded) with a real, stated default and maximum page size; the unbounded behavior is gone, not just capped high enough to dodge today's specific 5s timeout. A regression test seeds more than one page's worth of real rows and asserts the response only returns one page, with real pagination metadata (total count or next-cursor). `blackbox-exporter`'s own probe target updated to match the new paginated shape if the URL/response contract changes. A live check confirms the new endpoint's real response time and size for a first-page request, not assumed from the code alone.
- Dependencies: none.
- Priority: P1. Labels: `backend`, `bug`, `platform`. A real, live-confirmed OOM risk on a production-adjacent service, not a routine enhancement — prioritized above this section's usual P2 default for exactly that reason. **Done (2026-08-14, services#70/platform#171)**: the theoretical risk became a real, active incident before this item was picked up — `api` had restarted 282 times over 41 hours (`OOMKilled`, confirmed via `lastState.terminated`). Real driver, corrected by review after this item's own fix PRs (services#70/platform#171) first misattributed it to `workload-generator`'s ordinary `work_item_read` traffic: that path normally hits the bounded per-id `GET /work-items/{id}` (`run.py`'s `client.get_work_item`), only falling back to the unbounded list on a cold `created_ids` cache, and a real Loki query across the whole incident window found zero `action="work_item_list"` lines from `workload-generator` — that fallback was essentially unreachable once the generator's own writes had filled its cache, which happened within seconds of a 2026-08-10 startup, days before this incident. The actual driver was `blackbox-exporter`'s own recurring authenticated probe (`http_2xx_auth`, `platform/argocd/apps/prometheus.yaml`) hitting this same unbounded endpoint every 1 minute — 41h × 60 ≈ 2,460 attempts, comfortably consistent with 282 real kills (~11% hit rate) — which is also exactly what this item's own original Purpose paragraph above named as the discovery mechanism in the first place. `GET /work-items` now takes `?page`/`?size` (default 50, hard ceiling 200 a caller can't bypass with a larger `?size=`), returns a `WorkItemPage` envelope instead of a bare array, ordered by `createdAt` descending. Review caught a real test-isolation bug before merge (two `@Test` methods sharing one Testcontainers Postgres with no cleanup between them, seeded rows leaking across tests, confirmed via the actual CI failure log) — fixed with a `@BeforeEach` truncate. No `platform`-side probe change needed: confirmed live that `blackbox-exporter`'s `http_2xx_auth`/`http_401_expected` modules only check HTTP status codes, no body assertion, and `workload-generator`'s own client only reads `response.status_code`, never the body. Deployed live (platform#171) through the existing canary rollout — unlike the prior #122 deploy, the analysis passed cleanly this time (no `status.promoteFull` needed). Verified live end to end: `GET /work-items` now returns `200`, `5.7KB`, `149ms` (was ~14MB, routinely timing out past blackbox's 5s budget) against a real `142,338`-row table; the new pod has run with `0` restarts since; `ApiHighErrorRate`, `ApiVariantsLookupHighErrorRate`, and `BlackboxProbeFailing` for this target are all confirmed cleared in Prometheus's own `/api/v1/alerts`.

**131. Real node-wide memory pressure kills workloads independent of their own container limits — found live closing out #122**
- Purpose: closing #122's `ClinVarIngestionFreshnessBreach` required triggering one real, successful ingestion to give the fixed counter its first genuine increment. The first live attempt SIGKILLed the pod (exit 137) right at its old 768Mi container limit — a real, legitimate per-container sizing gap, fixed (platform#170, 768Mi->1536Mi, verified via `container_memory_working_set_bytes`). A second live attempt, on the resized pod, was **also** SIGKILLed — but this time peaking at only 765.4MiB, well under the new 1536Mi ceiling. That rules out the container's own cgroup limit as the cause: the real host (`free -h` on the node itself, single-node cluster, ADR 0040) showed 12Gi/19Gi used and **5Gi of swap already active at rest**, with `kubectl describe node` independently confirming `limits.memory` already ~110-114% overcommitted cluster-wide even before this bump. The most likely real mechanism: the kernel's own OOM killer, triggered by aggregate node-wide memory pressure (not any single cgroup's own limit), picked the fastest-growing RSS process at that moment — clinvar-service's ingestion burst — rather than the process that was actually over its own declared budget. A false lead was checked and ruled out honestly rather than left ambiguous: `workers`' pod also showed liveness/readiness failures in the same window, which looked like collateral damage at first glance, but `kubectl get deployment -n workers` shows `0/0` replicas with a `keda-hpa-workers` HPA present — that was KEDA's own normal idle scale-to-zero cycle (ADR 0009/0011), unrelated to memory pressure, not a second victim.
- Acceptance Criteria: a real accounting of the node's steady-state memory baseline (`kubectl top pods -A --sort-by=memory` plus the host's own `free -h`/`swapon --show`) recorded, not assumed — is 5Gi of resident swap normal/expected for this project's real day-to-day footprint (30+ real co-scheduled workloads on one laptop node, ADR 0040) or itself a sign something is oversized/leaking and worth its own investigation. A decision recorded on whether memory-heavy bursty workloads (this ClinVar ingestion; any future similar batch job) need to be scheduling-aware of concurrent node load (e.g., avoid triggering admin-initiated bursts during other real batch windows) or whether the real fix is trimming steady-state consumption elsewhere (the 2026-08-11 review's already-flagged, still-open Mimir OOM-restart pattern is a plausible contributor, worth checking first rather than assumed). Whatever the conclusion, a real, triggered ClinVar ingestion completes successfully end to end at least once, proven live (not just "should work now"), and `ClinVarIngestionFreshnessBreach` is confirmed actually cleared, not just theoretically fixable.
- Dependencies: #122.
- Priority: P1. Labels: `platform`, `observability`, `bug`. **Done (2026-08-14, ADR 0041 + #132)** — the measurement/decision half was met first: a real 4-day trend of aggregate container memory usage (6.8-8.3GiB, flat) rules out a leak, `kubectl describe node`/`free -h` give a real, recorded baseline (requests 46% but real usage 59-63%, limits 114% overcommitted, 4.4-5Gi of 8Gi swap resident at rest), and a decision is recorded: this is accepted as a real, known operating constraint on this hardware (same treatment ADR 0040 gave CPU), not chased as a bug. The "ingestion succeeds live" half took two more real fixes in #132 (application-level, not node-level) before it was met — see #132's own resolution and #122's final note for the full account. A fifth real attempt, after both of #132's fixes, succeeded: `0` restarts, `clinvar_ingestion_jobs_total{status="succeeded"}=1`, `ClinVarIngestionFreshnessBreach` confirmed cleared live.

**132. ClinVar ingestion's peak memory needs to actually come down, not just get more retries**
- Purpose: three consecutive real, live attempts to trigger a ClinVar ingestion (backlog #131, closing out #122) were all SIGKILLed by node-wide memory pressure — not the container's own limit, which was raised once already (platform#170, 768Mi->1536Mi) and never came close to being hit on any of the three attempts (767, 765, 906 MiB peaks, all under half the ceiling on two of three). ADR 0041 records that this node's real memory is chronically tight enough that "wait for a better moment" doesn't reliably have anywhere better to arrive at — checked live, not assumed. The next scheduled cron run (`CLINVAR_INGESTION_CRON`) will very likely hit the exact same wall unattended, with nobody watching it fail. The real, credible fix is reducing how much memory a single ingestion run actually needs at its peak, not hoping for a quieter moment or repeatedly retrying the same request against an unchanged ceiling.
- Acceptance Criteria: `services/clinvar-service/app/ingestion.py`'s real peak memory during a full ingestion is measured (not assumed) and the dominant contributor identified — the leading real candidate is buffering the full decompressed VCF/building the full tabix index in memory at once rather than a streaming approach, but this is to be confirmed against the actual code, not assumed from the outside. A real, live before/after comparison (`container_memory_working_set_bytes` during a real triggered ingestion, same method #131 used) shows a materially lower peak than the ~900MiB-1GiB range observed across the three failed attempts. A real ingestion completes successfully end to end at least once against the current node, proven live, and `ClinVarIngestionFreshnessBreach` is confirmed actually cleared in Prometheus's own `/api/v1/alerts` — the concrete proof this item and #131/#122 have all been chasing.
- Dependencies: #131.
- Priority: P1. Labels: `backend`, `bug`, `platform`, `observability`. **Done (2026-08-14, services#71/#72, platform#172/#173)** — two real, distinct, live-measured contributors, not one: `_build_variant_index_rows` (`app/ingestion.py`) accumulated a real run's entire ~2.9M variant-index rows in memory before a single bulk insert, fixed by streaming inserts in bounded 20,000-row batches (services#71) plus a real correctness follow-through (FK-safe cleanup on cancellation/orphan reconciliation, and scoping rsID lookups to the active release only — both real gaps caught by review before merge). A fourth real attempt confirmed that fix working (scan phase flat at ~88MiB across a full real run, down from ~700-900MiB) but still failed right after the scan finished, isolating the second contributor: `compute_changed_keys` (`app/diff.py`) building two full ~4.4M-entry in-memory dicts for the release diff — already self-flagged in that file's own comment as a known optimization target. Fixed by streaming the diff against one dict instead of two (services#72), correctness-analyzed and fuzz-tested (20,000 trials) by the reviewing agent, with one narrow, explicitly-documented, accepted edge case (a same-key duplicate within a single VCF file) rather than a silent gap. The fifth real attempt, after both fixes deployed, succeeded end to end — `clinvar_ingestion_jobs_total{status="succeeded"}=1` via a real scrape, `0` restarts, `ClinVarIngestionFreshnessBreach` confirmed cleared in Prometheus's own `/api/v1/alerts`.

**133. `market-data-ingestor`'s watchdog can't tell "connected" from "actually receiving real trades" — found live, ~29 real hours silent**
- Purpose: closing out backlog #122/#131/#132 surfaced two other real, currently-firing alerts unrelated to that incident. `MarketDataStaleFeed` was firing for all 5 watchlisted tickers (`AAPL`/`MSFT`/`GOOGL`/`AMZN`/`TSLA`), confirmed live via `market_data_seconds_since_last_tick`: **~29 real hours** since the last genuine trade for every single ticker, during real US market hours (backlog #78's own `MarketHoursService`, Mon-Fri 09:30-16:00 America/New_York, correctly gating — this wasn't a false after-hours positive). Real root cause, found by reading `FinnhubWebSocketClient`'s own watchdog: it only tracks `lastFrameReceivedAt`, updated by *any* frame including Finnhub's own protocol-level ping/pong keep-alives — a connection can pass that liveness check indefinitely while never delivering a real `"trade"` message again. Confirmed live, not assumed: triggering `POST /internal/market-data/force-reconnect` (the admin endpoint backlog #78 already built for exactly this kind of test) fixed it within seconds — all 5 tickers had a fresh real tick (0-12s old) shortly after. The daily ~24h reconnect cycle already visible in this pod's own logs (Finnhub closes the connection from its own side roughly once a day) evidently reconnected the *socket* without ever restoring real trade delivery, and nothing was watching for that distinction until a human happened to notice the alert.
- Acceptance Criteria: the watchdog gains a second, real signal distinct from protocol-level frame liveness — whether any watchlisted ticker has gone stale by `StaleFeedMetrics`' own already-alerting-on definition (same threshold, same market-hours gating, no second staleness definition to keep in sync) — and force-reconnects when it has, with a real cooldown so a reconnect that hasn't yet had time to receive a fresh tick (0-12s observed live) doesn't reconnect-storm, and a genuinely persistent (e.g. real upstream Finnhub outage) condition still retries periodically rather than backing off forever. A live check after deploy confirms `MarketDataStaleFeed` stays cleared without needing another manual `force-reconnect` call across at least one real instance of the existing ~24h reconnect cycle.
- Dependencies: none.
- Priority: P1. Labels: `backend`, `bug`, `observability`. A real, live, ~29-hour-silent data gap a human had to notice and manually fix — not a hypothetical.

**134. `ArgoCDAppOutOfSync` firing since 2026-08-12 — two real, unrelated, now-fixed causes**
- Purpose: found live continuing past #133 — `ArgoCDAppOutOfSync` had been firing since `2026-08-12T17:34:01Z`, unrelated to backlog #122/#131/#132/#133. Two genuinely separate causes behind the two `OutOfSync` Applications, neither assumed, both confirmed live: (1) `prometheus-network-policies`'s `CiliumNetworkPolicy/prometheus-server` reported `OutOfSync` while `kubectl diff` (a real server-side dry-run against the git-tracked manifest — the actual ground truth, not ArgoCD's own cached comparison) showed **zero** real difference; a real `kubectl.kubernetes.io/last-applied-configuration` annotation left over from an earlier live `kubectl apply` (this same NetworkPolicy, backlog #122's `argo-rollouts` ingress-gap incident, `platform`#167) was a red herring — removing it didn't clear the status, but a genuine ArgoCD sync operation did, confirming this was a stale comparison cache, not a real drift, ever. (2) `cilium`'s three Hubble/CA TLS Secrets (`cilium-ca`, `hubble-relay-client-certs`, `hubble-server-certs`) are `OutOfSync` because the chart's default `hubble.tls.auto.method: helm` regenerates a fresh self-signed cert via Helm's own `genSelfSignedCert` on every render — a well-documented real Helm/ArgoCD interaction (non-deterministic template output), not a bug in these Secrets or a real configuration drift to chase down.
- Acceptance Criteria: `prometheus-network-policies` re-synced and confirmed `Synced` live. `cilium`'s Application gains a real `ignoreDifferences` entry scoping to just these three Secrets' `data` field (the standard, documented ArgoCD pattern for chart-managed non-deterministic cert regeneration), confirmed live to bring `cilium` back to `Synced` without touching the certs' own generation method. Switching to `hubble.tls.auto.method: certmanager` (cert-manager is already deployed in this cluster, would remove the regeneration entirely) explicitly recorded as real future scope, not attempted here — a change to Hubble's own mTLS material deserves its own deliberate verification, not a same-pass side effect of an alert-noise cleanup.
- Dependencies: none.
- Priority: P2. Labels: `platform`, `observability`. Real alert noise masking any future genuine `ArgoCDAppOutOfSync` firing (a real drift would be invisible in a sea of two permanently-expected ones) — worth fixing on its own merits, not just cosmetic.
  **Done (2026-08-14, platform#176/#177, adamastorx#264)** — both real causes fixed and verified live, note added late (2026-08-15) after the fix itself shipped without a closing marker on this item. `prometheus-network-policies` re-synced (`kubectl patch application ... sync`), confirmed `Synced` live. `cilium.yaml` gained the real `ignoreDifferences` entry (3 Secrets, scoped to `/data`), confirmed live to bring `cilium` to `Synced` without touching cert generation — required syncing the parent `root` app-of-apps Application first before the child `cilium` Application's own updated spec took effect, a real ArgoCD sync-ordering gotcha found live. Both Applications confirmed `Synced`/`Healthy`, zero `ArgoCDAppOutOfSync` alerts firing in Prometheus's own `/api/v1/alerts`. Switching to `hubble.tls.auto.method: certmanager` remains real, deliberately-deferred future scope, unchanged from this item's own AC.

---

## Simplification

The project's owner asked, for the first time, for the *opposite* of every
prior (additive) review: for a personal SRE/platform **portfolio** — no
real users, no revenue — what should this project **stop doing, remove, or
never have built** because it doesn't serve that goal? Sunk cost is not a
reason to keep anything; a piece earns its place only by whether its
complexity pays off *for this audience*. These items are concrete removals,
each with a done state. None deletes code by itself — every actual deletion
stays a separate, explicitly-confirmed owner decision, taken one at a time.
ADR 0021 records the significant, hard-to-reverse ones (remove a whole
service, drop a domain, close a milestone).

Guiding cut/keep calls (the reasoning; the actions are S1–S7 below):
- **Keep** `clinvar-service`, its dedicated Postgres, the ClinVar release
  provenance, and invalidation-on-write — the polyglot judgement call (ADR
  0019) and the provenance story are this project's single most defensible
  portfolio content. The two-Postgres/two-CI/two-base-image tax is the
  *load-bearing consequence* of that decision (namespace-scoped Secrets/PVCs
  can't cross a boundary — the lesson learned twice), not incidental cost;
  it stays because the thing it supports stays.
- **Cut** everything that is infrastructure maintaining zero real function
  (`gateway`, `whoami`), breadth that adds no new SRE signal (gnomAD, HGVS,
  liftover, chaos scenarios 4–7), or enterprise/multi-team ritual that a
  single operator generating their own traffic cannot meaningfully exercise
  (multi-window burn-rate budgets, solo "blameless" postmortems, a laptop
  load-test "capacity baseline", a single-node "node-loss game-day").

**S1. Remove the `gateway` service entirely; expose `api` directly**
- Purpose: `gateway` has exactly one route — `GET /api/hello` → `api`'s
  `GET /hello`, an M1 placeholder (ADR 0010) — never wired to any real
  traffic (`work-items`/`/variants/lookup` all hit `api` directly), zero
  real requests since last restart. It nonetheless carries a full,
  permanent tax: its own Spring Boot module, CI build/scan/publish matrix
  entry (`services/.github/workflows/ci.yml` + `build-publish.yml`,
  `GatewayMetricsHistogramTest`), Kubernetes namespace/Deployment/Service,
  ArgoCD Application, Traefik Ingress, and TLS certificate — the exact
  shape of "healthy infrastructure maintaining zero real function." The
  ADR 0010 rationale ("single entrypoint for future auth/aggregation/
  rate-limiting") never materialised; a two-method forwarder in front of
  one backend does not earn a service, an image, a pipeline, and a cert.
- Acceptance Criteria: `services/gateway` deleted, including its CI matrix
  entries and `GatewayMetricsHistogramTest`; `platform/kubernetes/gateway/`
  and `argocd/apps/gateway.yaml` deleted; the `gateway` namespace gone.
  `api` gets its own Ingress + cert-manager certificate (the same
  `adamastorx-ca` pattern gateway/whoami used) so a real service is the
  live Traefik+TLS+service path; verified reachable through Traefik with
  TLS. ADR 0010 marked `Superseded by 0021`. Every doc/manifest that
  enumerates `gateway` reconciled in the same change: drop its rows from
  #21/#21a's SLO+histogram tables, from #35's CPU-limit list, and from any
  golden-signal dashboard/runbook. If a real cross-cutting edge concern
  ever arrives, reintroducing an edge service (or Traefik middleware) is a
  fresh, deliberate decision — not a reason to keep an empty one now.
- Dependencies: none. ADR 0021.
- Priority: P1. Labels: `platform`, `backend`, `architecture`.

**S2. Remove the `whoami` proof app**
- Purpose: `traefik/whoami` was M1's end-to-end proof that Traefik +
  cert-manager issue and serve TLS. Real services exist now; that proof no
  longer needs a dedicated app with its own namespace, `ResourceQuota`,
  `LimitRange`, Ingress, certificate, and ArgoCD Application. Once S1 gives
  `api` an Ingress, a real service is the standing ingress+TLS proof.
- Acceptance Criteria: `platform/kubernetes/whoami/` and
  `argocd/apps/whoami.yaml` deleted; the `whoami` namespace gone; a real
  service's Ingress (per S1) documented as the ingress+cert-manager proof
  in `docs/architecture/overview.md` and `.claude/PROJECT.md` (which both
  currently cite `whoami` as that proof).
- Dependencies: S1 (so a real Ingress exists before whoami's is removed).
- Priority: P2. Labels: `platform`.

**S3. Drop gnomAD enrichment; make ClinVar the sole annotation source — DONE**
- Purpose: gnomAD was never built; its real footprint (~7.7GB, per
  `SESSION_STATE.md`, not the "few hundred MB" ADR 0018 assumed) does not
  fit this single-node cluster, and it adds a *second* data source that
  introduces no operational signal ClinVar does not already provide — pure
  breadth for a bio audience, not depth for an SRE one. #24a already
  concedes deprioritising it entirely is a valid outcome; this makes that
  the decision.
- Acceptance Criteria: gnomAD removed from #24's acceptance criteria (the
  endpoint returns ClinVar clinical significance + release id only); ADR
  0018's scope amended to ClinVar-only with a note pointing to ADR 0021;
  the gnomAD section of `docs/data-sources.md` removed or marked
  out-of-scope; #24a closed as resolved (answer: no gnomAD). `clinvar-
  service`, its Postgres, the provenance story, and invalidation-on-write
  are untouched.
- Dependencies: none. ADR 0021.
- Priority: P2. Labels: `backend`, `architecture`.

**S4. Close M6 (#30); stop reserving the FASTQ/alignment milestone — DONE**
- Purpose: #30 reserves a real alignment pipeline on self-hosted MinIO +
  Kubernetes Jobs — a whole new object-storage/batch data plane built to
  serve bioinformatics depth for a bio audience, not SRE/platform depth.
  The genuinely distinctive portfolio asset (the polyglot pivot and the
  provenance story) is already delivered by `clinvar-service`; M6 extends
  the "bio-flavored coat of paint," which is the exact failure mode #30 was
  raised to guard against — going deeper into it doesn't fix it. For a
  simplification pass, closing the placeholder beats reserving it.
- Acceptance Criteria: #30 marked won't-do (struck through, like #27) with
  this reasoning inline; ADR 0018's and ADR 0019's forward references to
  #30/M6 annotated as closed (see ADR 0021); #30's sequencing note about
  #38/#39 folded into those items or dropped. No open backlog item or ADR
  still treats M6 as committed future work.
- Dependencies: none. ADR 0021.
- Priority: P2. Labels: `architecture`.

**S5. Cut the M5 bioinformatics-depth enhancements #40 (HGVS) and #41 (liftover) — DONE**
- Purpose: HGVS-input support and GRCh37→GRCh38 liftover add
  clinical-genomics surface for a bio audience; they teach an SRE/platform
  reviewer nothing new once rsID + coordinate + normalized lookups already
  work. They are the same "bio coat of paint goes deeper" pattern as M6.
  #38 (a real correctness bug — silent `LIMIT 1` data loss) and #39 (the
  cheapest, highest-signal correctness fix — indel normalization) stay:
  they are real bugs, not depth-for-depth's-sake.
- Acceptance Criteria: #40 and #41 marked won't-do with this reasoning
  inline; #38 and #39 explicitly retained. No item still lists #40/#41 as
  in-scope work.
- Dependencies: none.
- Priority: P2. Labels: `backend`, `architecture`.

**S6. Trim the chaos plan (#23) from seven scenarios to three — DONE**
- Purpose: #23's seven scenarios each demand a full signal → dashboard →
  alert → runbook → fault-proof → recovery-proof → article fact-pack. For a
  single operator, three well-chosen scenarios already prove the alerts and
  runbooks work; scenarios 4–7 (poison-message/DLT, readiness probe,
  ArgoCD drift, `clinvar-service` PVC) re-exercise the same muscle without
  teaching a new SRE skill or a new class of signal — breadth that reads as
  thoroughness but returns little once the first three are done.
- Acceptance Criteria: #23's acceptance criteria reduced to three
  scenarios — (1) Kafka broker unavailable, (2) PostgreSQL unavailable /
  PVC full, (3) consumer-group lag — each still producing the full
  signal/dashboard/alert/runbook/proof loop. Scenarios 4–7 dropped (a
  one-line note may record that any one of them is a fine *ad-hoc* future
  exercise, not a committed deliverable).
- Dependencies: none.
- Priority: P2. Labels: `observability`, `platform`.

**S7. Cut the M4 résumé-padding items: close #21b, #33, #34; merge #23b into #23a — DONE**
- Purpose: four M4 items add ceremony a single operator can't meaningfully
  exercise, on top of work that already covers the real ground.
  - **#21b (error-budget policy + multi-window burn-rate alerts)** is an
    enterprise, multi-team SRE ritual; you cannot meaningfully "burn" a
    budget against traffic you generate yourself, and a "change freeze
    until budget recovers" is theatre for a solo repo. #21's per-SLO alerts
    are the right altitude and the actual teaching moment.
  - **#33 (blameless postmortems for the three real incidents)** duplicates
    what ADRs 0018/0019 and `SESSION_STATE.md` already record; a solo
    "blameless" postmortem is a solo writeup. The useful part (surfacing
    the incidents as portfolio narrative) is already #31's job.
  - **#34 (k6/vegeta capacity baseline)** load-tests self-generated traffic
    on one laptop node — it measures the laptop, not a capacity baseline;
    the ~90s ingestion figure is already a real measured number, and #21's
    thresholds can cite it directly.
  - **#23b (node-loss/DR game-day)** on a single-node laptop cluster is
    ceremony: #23a already documents single-node/disk loss as an accepted,
    stated risk and proves a `pg_dump`-based restore once. Its one useful
    ask (record real measured RTO/RPO from the restore) folds into #23a.
- Acceptance Criteria: #21b, #33, #34 marked won't-do with the reasoning
  above inline; #23b's RTO/RPO-measurement ask merged into #23a and #23b
  itself closed. Genuinely-worth-keeping M4 work is stated explicitly as
  retained: #21 (SLOs/alerts), #21c (real notification channel — done),
  #21d (disk-full alert — cheap, real single-node failure mode), #21e
  (real metric gap for #21), #22 (runbooks), #23 (trimmed per S6), #23a
  (backup/restore — a real, uncovered gap), #36 (root-cause Secret fix),
  #37 (rollback runbook — done). #35 (per-namespace CPU limits) stays for
  the limits themselves, but its "CI check that fails a PR with no CPU
  limit" is downgraded to optional — a lint gate is gold-plating for a
  solo repo where review already catches it.
- Dependencies: none.
- Priority: P2. Labels: `observability`, `platform`, `documentation`.

**42. Real Kafka broker/topic availability alert**
- Purpose: chaos scenario 1 (`observability/chaos/01-kafka-broker-unavailable.md`, backlog #23) ran a real Kafka outage live and found none of the 6 existing alert rules fired — `ApiHighErrorRate` needs a sustained 5-minute window of non-zero real traffic at >5% error rate, and a brief outage under this project's actual low/manual-test traffic pattern never sustains that. There is currently no alert that detects "Kafka itself is unavailable or missing a topic" directly, only ones that infer it from downstream error rates that need real traffic to trip.
- Acceptance Criteria: a Prometheus alert on Kafka's own availability (e.g. `up{job=~"kafka.*"}` if scraped, or a broker/topic health check) fires within a reasonable window of a real outage, independent of whether `api`/`workers` are receiving traffic at the time. Verified live against a repeated version of chaos scenario 1.
- Dependencies: #21.
- Priority: P2 (downgraded from P1, backlog #47, 2026-07-31). Labels: `observability`. **Reassessed, not closed**: re-running scenario 1 under #45's permanent real traffic showed `ApiHighErrorRate` now fires unaided in ~8m44s against this cluster's actual Kafka failure mode (broker restart → ephemeral-storage topic loss → sustained downstream errors) — the gap this item was scoped against no longer exists for that shape. What it would still catch that `ApiHighErrorRate` cannot: a Kafka outage that resolves before 5 minutes of elevated error rate accumulate (this cluster's own ephemeral storage doesn't currently produce that shape, but a real broker/topic health alert would generalize to it). Kept as real, lower-priority defense-in-depth rather than closed outright.
  **The AC's own alert now exists (2026-08-15, platform#178)**: `KafkaBrokerUnavailable` (a real TCP `blackbox-kafka-tcp` probe against `kafka.kafka.svc.cluster.local:9092`, `for: 2m`, `severity: critical`) fires on broker unavailability directly, independent of `api`/`workers` traffic — platform#178's own commit message and in-file comments cite this item's number directly, built deliberately for this AC, not a repurposed side effect of other work.
  **Done, with the gap named in the label itself (2026-08-15)** — deliberately not a bare "Done": the required live re-run happened, owner-approved: same fault (`kubectl scale statefulset kafka-controller -n kafka --replicas=0`), full writeup in `observability/chaos/01-kafka-broker-unavailable.md`'s own 2026-08-15 postscript. Real result, not the scripted outcome assumed: the alert did **not** fire this time — the real outage window was ~60s (queried directly from Prometheus's raw `probe_success` series, the last confirmed-`1` sample to the first confirmed-`1` sample after; an honest bound, not a precise duration, given the job's own real 1-minute scrape cadence), under the `for: 2m` threshold, and root-caused further to that same real 1-minute scrape interval (`/api/v1/targets`) — a structural detection-resolution limit a shorter `for:` alone wouldn't fix. The AC's literal text asked for the alert to *fire* on a real outage; on the only real outage shape this cluster currently produces, it didn't. A second, unplanned finding in the same run: recovery is now fully automatic end-to-end — a `kafka-provisioning` Job recreated every real topic with zero manual `kafka-topics.sh` intervention (unlike both 2026-07-26 and 2026-07-31's runs), proven with a real `POST /work-items` → `202` → `workers` (KEDA 0→1) actually consuming it, confirmed in Loki. Decision recorded rather than left open, after seeing the real result, not before: a sub-minute self-resolving blip that's already faster than a human page-and-react cycle isn't worth tightening the scrape interval for, given `ApiHighErrorRate` already covers the slower, worse real failure shape (#47's 2026-07-31 postscript).
